The function encodes these positions in compressed sparse\n//   row (CSR) format.\n//\n// Arguments:\n//   cols:          array for column indexes of elements (size should be = n)\n//   rowDelimiters: array of size dim+1 holding indices to rows;\n//                  last element is the index one past the last element\n//   n:             number of nonzero elements\n//   dim:           number of rows/columns in the matrix\n//\n// ****************************************************************************\nvoid initRandomMatrix(index_t* cols, index_t* rowDelimiters, const index_t n, const index_t dim) {\n    index_t nnzAssigned = 0;\n\n    // Figure out the probability that a nonzero should be assigned\n    double prob = static_cast<double>(n) / (static_cast<double>(dim) * static_cast<double>(dim));\n\n    // Seed random number generator\n    srand(8675309);\n\n    // Randomly decide whether entry i,j gets a value, but ensure n values are assigned\n    bool fillRemaining = false;\n    for (index_t i = 0; i < dim; ++i) {\n        rowDelimiters[i] = nnzAssigned;\n        for (index_t j = 0; j < dim; ++j) {\n            index_t numEntriesLeft = (dim * dim) - ((i * dim) + j);\n            index_t needToAssign = n - nnzAssigned;\n            if (numEntriesLeft <= needToAssign) {\n                fillRemaining = true;\n            }\n            double randVal = static_cast<double>(rand()) / RAND_MAX;\n            if ((nnzAssigned < n && randVal <= prob) || fillRemaining) {\n                // Assign (i,j) a value\n                cols[nnzAssigned] = j;\n                nnzAssigned++;\n            }\n        }\n    }\n    // Convention: put the number of non-zeroes at the end of the row delimiters array\n    rowDelimiters[dim] = n;\n}\n\n// ****************************************************************************\n// Function: spmvCpu\n//\n// Purpose:\n//   Computes sparse matrix-vector multiplication using CSR format\n//\n// Arguments:\n//   val: array holding the non-zero values for the matrix\n//   cols: array of column indices for each element\n//   rowDelimiters: array of size dim+1 holding indices to rows;\n//                  last element is the index one past the last element\n//   vec: dense vector of size dim to be used for multiplication\n//   dim: number of rows/columns in the matrix\n//   out: output - result from the spmv calculation\n//\n// ****************************************************************************\nvoid spmvCpu(const double* val, const index_t* cols, const index_t* rowDelimiters,\n             const double* vec, const index_t dim, double* out) {\n    #pragma omp parallel for schedule(static)\n    for (index_t i = 0; i < dim; ++i) {\n        double t = 0.0;\n        for (index_t j = rowDelimiters[i]; j < rowDelimiters[i + 1]; ++j) {\n            const auto col = cols[j];\n            t += val[j] * vec[col];\n        }\n        out[i] = t;\n    }\n}\n\n// ****************************************************************************\n// Function: verifyResults\n//\n// Purpose:\n//   Verifies correctness of results by comparing to reference solution\n//\n// Arguments:\n//   reference: array holding the reference result vector\n//   result: array holding the result vector to verify\n//   size: number of elements per vector\n//\n// ****************************************************************************\nbool verifyResults(const double* reference, const double* result, const index_t size) {\n    for (index_t i = 0; As noted, lane0 can use shuffles. Let's estimate performance: each warp processes one row. For row length L~100, loop over chunks of 32:\n- each lane loads val[j+lane], cols[j+lane], vec[col] -> coalesced-ish (val contiguous, cols contiguous, vec scattered).\n- compute product p = val*vec[col] (non-FMA? separate).\n- lane0 then for k=0..31 gets p from lane k via __shfl_sync and adds sequentially. But wait this changes order only within chunk? We need strict global order across all elements: within chunk, add lane0..lane31 in order, then next chunk. Yes exactly sequential order! This preserves exact order! And loads are parallel/coalesced. The additions are done by lane0 sequentially, but that's inherent. The key benefit: memory loads coalesced and parallelized across lanes, and more warps active because warp per row. But each warp's additions are serialized on lane0 (only lane0 doing work during reduction), so arithmetic throughput 1/32 of warp. Yet SpMV is memory-bound; arithmetic per element one mul+add. With many warps, GPU can still issue adds from many lane0s? A warp instruction executes for all lanes, but only lane0's result matters; the add still occupies the warp. So arithmetic throughput per warp is 1 element per instruction vs 32 for normal. GPU ALU throughput becomes bottleneck: 1 FMA/cycle/warp * 82 SMs * 4 schedulers... Let's compute: 82 SMs * 4 warps/cycle * 1 element * ~1.5GHz = 492G elements/s if add+shfl etc maybe 1/4 -> ~120G elem/s. Current one-thread-per-row with 4096 threads: maybe limited by memory. Warp approach could be faster due to occupancy/coalescing.\n\nBut implementing shuffle-based ordered reduction: Need lane0 to fetch each lane's product. Use `__shfl_sync(mask, p, k)` for k=0..31 (all lanes execute shfl, but only lane0 uses result; still instruction). Then sum += shfl result. That's 32 shfl + 32 add = 64 instructions per 32 elements = 2 instr/element, plus loads/mul. One-thread kernel: ~1 add + 1 mul + loads per element. So this is ~4x more instructions but 32x more threads. For memory-bound, coalesced loads help. Could be net faster or comparable. But complexity and uncertain.\n\nEven better: Use warp-cooperative loads into shared memory, then lane0 sums from shared sequentially. Loads coalesced, sums by lane0. Similar.\n\nAlternatively, use \"thread per row\" but improve parallelism by using multiple threads per row with independent accumulators, then compute final sum in an order chosen to minimize error, not exact. That's bad: MPI scaling will not improve because work per GPU shrinks and occupancy drops. With warp-per-row, total warps = rows, per GPU warps = rows/G, still decreases, but each warp has 32 threads, total threads 32*rows/G. Scaling still limited by total parallelism 32*rows vs GPUs.
